                      - text: were used in conjunction with QS to degrade targeting mRNAs, enabling dynamic regulation of the shikimate pathway increasing para-hydroxybenzoic acid (PHBA) production (
                      - link "Williams et al., 2015" [ref=e451] [cursor=pointer]:
                        - /url: "#R121"
                      - text: ) (
                      - link "Fig. 3D" [ref=e452] [cursor=pointer]:
                        - /url: "#F3"
                      - text: ).
                  - generic [ref=e453]:
                    - heading "3.2.3. Post-translation actuation" [level=4] [ref=e454]
                    - paragraph [ref=e455]:
                      - text: Post-translational control of enzyme activity by a metabolite of interest can provide rapid control over the flux of a pathway and can be ideal for dynamic metabolic systems (
                      - link "Venayak et al., 2015" [ref=e456] [cursor=pointer]:
                        - /url: "#R115"
                      - text: ). However, engineering allosteric enzymes for specific reactions is extremely challenging because novel allosteric interactions require high selectivity and robust output upon metabolite binding. Existing post-translation actuation mechanisms mostly focus on regulating enzyme degradation rate by adding degradation tags to target enzymes. For example, a TevP protease-based dynamic regulation circuit was constructed to create an ON and OFF switch that accumulates and degrades target proteins, respectively, depending on the degradation tags used. The protease was placed under growth- or stationary-phase promoters, allowing the system to respond to the growth stage and improving shikimate production (
                      - link "Gao et al., 2019" [ref=e457] [cursor=pointer]:
                        - /url: "#R38"
                      - text: ) (
                      - link "Fig. 3E" [ref=e458] [cursor=pointer]:
                        - /url: "#F3"
                      - text: ).
              - generic [ref=e459]:
                - heading "4. Applications of dynamic metabolic control" [level=2] [ref=e460]: 4. Applications of dynamic metabolic control
                - paragraph [ref=e461]:
                  - text: As theories and molecular biology tools are developed, an increasing number of dynamic metabolic control systems were developed for numerous pathways to benefit bioproduction (
                  - link "Table 1" [ref=e462] [cursor=pointer]:
                    - /url: "#T1"
                  - text: ). In this section, we will summarize the pathways and metabolic nodes being dynamically regulated.
                - generic [ref=e463]:
                  - heading "Table 1." [level=3] [ref=e464]
                  - paragraph [ref=e466]:
                    - text: Dynamic metabolic control in metabolic engineering.

                        - /url: "#TFN1"

                        - cell "1.8-fold (titer)" [ref=e992]
                        - cell "Lv et al. (2020)" [ref=e993]:
                          - link "Lv et al. (2020)" [ref=e994] [cursor=pointer]:
                            - /url: "#R74"
                      - row "51 STR TS QS (GBL) TX Polyketide (rapamycin) 6.6-fold (titer) Tian et al. (2020)" [ref=e995]:

                          - link "Tian et al. (2020)" [ref=e1004] [cursor=pointer]:
                            - /url: "#R110"
                      - row "52 BS CM GlcN6P TX, CRISPRi GlcNAc 1.6-fold (titer) Wu et al. (2020)" [ref=e1005]:
                        - cell "52" [ref=e1006]
                        - cell "BS" [ref=e1007]

                          - link "Wu et al. (2020)" [ref=e1014] [cursor=pointer]:

                      - row "53 EC PB Tryptophan TX Tryptamine 2.1-fold (titer) Wang et al. (2020)" [ref=e1015]:
                        - cell "53" [ref=e1016]
                        - cell "EC" [ref=e1017]

                  - link "Open in a new tab" [ref=e1026] [cursor=pointer]:
                    - /url: table/T1/
                  - generic [ref=e1027]:
                    - generic [ref=e1028]:
                      - superscript [ref=e1029]: a
                      - paragraph [ref=e1030]: Examples using only traditional inducers are excluded. Examples are sorted by the year when the literature was published. In one literature, if a sensor-actuator system is applied to multiple pathways, these applications are considered as one example of dynamic metabolic control.
                    - generic [ref=e1031]:
                      - superscript [ref=e1032]: b
                      - paragraph [ref=e1033]:
                        - strong [ref=e1034]: Host abbreviations
                        - text: ": EC,"
                        - emphasis [ref=e1035]: Escherichia coli
                        - text: ; SC,
                        - emphasis [ref=e1036]: Saccharomyces cerevisiae
                        - text: ; CG,
                        - emphasis [ref=e1037]: Corynebacterium glutamicum
                        - text: ; AR,
                        - emphasis [ref=e1038]: Aspergillus niger
                        - text: ; BS,
                        - emphasis [ref=e1039]: Bacillus subtilis
                        - text: ; STR,
                        - emphasis [ref=e1040]: Streptomyces
                        - text: species; YL,
                        - emphasis [ref=e1041]: Yarrowia lipolytica
                        - text: .
                    - generic [ref=e1042]:
                      - superscript [ref=e1043]: c
                      - paragraph [ref=e1044]:
                        - strong [ref=e1045]: Strategy abbreviations
                        - text: ": TS, two-stage metabolic control; CM, continuous metabolic control; PB, population behavior control."
                    - generic [ref=e1046]:
                      - superscript [ref=e1047]: d
                      - paragraph [ref=e1048]:
                        - strong [ref=e1049]: Actuation level abbreviations
                        - text: ": TX, transcription; TL, translation; PD, protein degradation; asRNA, antisense RNA; GS, genetic sequence; CRISPRi, CRISPR interference; RNAi, RNA interference."
                    - generic [ref=e1050]:
                      - superscript [ref=e1051]: e
                      - paragraph [ref=e1052]:
                        - strong [ref=e1053]: Other abbreviations
                        - text: ": QS, quorum sensing; AHL, N-acyl homoserine lactones; GBL, γ-butyrolactones; ACP, acetyl phosphate; MI,"
                        - emphasis [ref=e1054]: myo
                        - text: "-inositol; MA, muconic acid; FAEE, fatty acid ethyl ester; PHBA, para-hydroxybenzoic acid; 3-HP, 3-hydroxypropionic acid; 4-HPAA, 4-hydroxyphenylacetic acid; PHB, poly-β-hydroxybutyrate; GlcN6P, glucosamine-6-phosphate; GlcNAc, N-acetylglucosamine; 4-HIL, 4-hydroxyisoleucine; 2-MBOH, 2-methyl-1-butanol; 4-HB, 4-hydroxybenzoate; PCA, protocatechuic acid; ALA, 5-aminolevulinic acid; ND, not detectable."
                - generic [ref=e1055]:
                  - heading "4.1. Applications in pathways branching from glycolysis" [level=3] [ref=e1056]
                  - paragraph [ref=e1057]:
                    - text: Glucaric acid is a precursor to detergents and nylons but has been proven difficult to produce in high quantities due to the carbon flux competition with glycolysis at the glucose-6-phosphate (G6P) branching point. To solve this competition, phosphofructokinase (PFK), the flux controlling enzyme in the upper glycolysis, needs to be repressed in the glucaric acid production phase. A QS system was used to achieve this dynamic repression once the cells reach a desired density, thereby switching from a growth phase to a production phase (
                    - link "Gupta et al., 2017" [ref=e1058] [cursor=pointer]:
                      - /url: "#R43"
                    - text: ). Another problem in this pathway is the low stability and activity of MI oxygenase (MIOX) (
                    - link "Fig. 4A" [ref=e1059] [cursor=pointer]:
                      - /url: "#F4"
                    - text: ). To delay the activity decline of MIOX, a MI biosensor was then used to upregulate the expression of MIOX upon the buildup of sufficient MI (
                    - link "Doong et al., 2018" [ref=e1060] [cursor=pointer]:
                      - /url: "#R31"
                    - text: ). The two dynamic control strategies collectively led to improved glucaric acid production at a titer of nearly 2 g/L in
                    - emphasis [ref=e1061]: E. coli
                    - text: .
                  - figure [ref=e1062]:
                    - heading "Fig. 4. Representative pathways with engineered metabolic regulation." [level=4] [ref=e1063]
                    - paragraph [ref=e1064]:
                      - img "Fig. 4." [ref=e1065]
                    - link "Open in a new tab" [ref=e1067] [cursor=pointer]:
                      - /url: figure/F4/
                    - paragraph [ref=e1069]:
                      - text: (A) Glucaric acid production in
                      - emphasis [ref=e1070]: E. coli
                      - text: (
                      - link "Doong et al., 2018" [ref=e1071] [cursor=pointer]:
                        - /url: "#R31"
                      - text: ). AHL, N-acyl homoserine lactones; PFK-1, phospho-fructokinase-1; G6P, glucose-6-ph osphate; F6P, fructose-6-phosphate; MI,
                      - emphasis [ref=e1072]: myo
                      - text: "-inositol; MIOX,"
                      - emphasis [ref=e1073]: myo
                      - text: "-inositol oxygenase. (B) 4-HIL production in"
                      - emphasis [ref=e1074]: C. glutamicum
                      - text: (
                      - link "Zhang et al., 2018" [ref=e1075] [cursor=pointer]:
                        - /url: "#R134"
                      - text: ). α-KG, α-ketoglutarate; SUCC, succinyl-CoA; SUC, succinate; OAA, oxaloacetate; Ile, L-isoleucine; 4-HIL, 4-hydroxyiso leucine; OdhA, α-ketoglutarate dehydrogenase complex E1 subunit. (C) MA production in
                      - emphasis [ref=e1076]: E. coli
                      - text: (
                      - link "Yang et al., 2018" [ref=e1077] [cursor=pointer]:
                        - /url: "#R130"
                      - text: ). E4P, erythrose 4-phosphate; PEP, phosphoenolpyruvate; DAHP, 3-deoxy-d-ara-bino-heptulosonate-7-phosphate; MA, muconic acid; AAA, aromatic amino acid; EntC, isochorismate synthase; PchB, isochorismate pyruvate lyase; PykF, pyruvate kinase I; PykA, pyruvate kinase II; Ppc, phosphoenolpyruvate carboxylase. (D) Naringenin production in
                      - emphasis [ref=e1078]: Y. lipolytica
                      - text: (
                      - link "Lv et al., 2020" [ref=e1079] [cursor=pointer]:
                        - /url: "#R74"
                      - text: ). LEU2, 3-isopropylmalate dehydrogenase; FAS1, fatty acid synthetases Fas1p; FAS2, fatty acid synthetases Fas2p; FabD, malonyl-CoA-ACP transacylase. β-IPM, 3-isopropylmalate; KIC, α-ketoi-socarpoate (E) Monoterpene production in
                      - emphasis [ref=e1080]: S. cerevisiae
                      - text: (
                      - link "Peng et al., 2018" [ref=e1081] [cursor=pointer]:
                        - /url: "#R88"
                      - text: ). DMAPP, dimethylallyl pyrophosphate; IPP, isopentenyl pyrophosphate; GPP, geranyl pyrophosphate; FPP, farnesol pyrophosphate; Erg20p, FPP synthase. (F) Xylonate production in
                      - emphasis [ref=e1082]: E. coli
                      - text: (
                      - link "Gao et al., 2020" [ref=e1083] [cursor=pointer]:
                        - /url: "#R37"
                      - text: ). PPP, pentose phosphate pathway; XylA, xylose isomerase; CcxylB, xylose dehydrogenase from
                      - emphasis [ref=e1084]: Caulobacter crescentus
                      - text: ; Dashed line, multiple enzymatic steps; blue line, engineered regulatory interaction; Circle, the signal molecule in the dynamic control system. Pathways and metabolites in bold are essential to the host.
                  - paragraph [ref=e1085]:
                    - text: Another important metabolic precursor from the central glycolysis is fructose-6-phosphate (F6P), which can be used to synthesize GlcNAc, a food supplement and nutraceutical. Dynamically downregulating PFK-1 in the glycolysis pathway and phosphoglucosamine mutase in the competitive peptidoglycan pathway was effective in GlcNAc overproduction (
                    - link "Niu et al., 2018" [ref=e1086] [cursor=pointer]:
                      - /url: "#R82"
                    - text: ;
                    - link "Wu et al., 2020" [ref=e1087] [cursor=pointer]:
                      - /url: "#R123"
                    - text: ). Controlling G6P dehydrogenase and GlcN6P N-acetyltransferase in response to the GlcN6P level has also been demonstrated to enhance GlcNAc production (
                    - link "Wu et al., 2020" [ref=e1088] [cursor=pointer]:
                      - /url: "#R123"
                    - text: ).
                - generic [ref=e1089]:
                  - heading "4.2. Applications in pathways branching from the TCA cycle" [level=3] [ref=e1090]
                  - paragraph [ref=e1091]:
                    - text: Many platform chemicals are synthesized from pyruvate. Meanwhile pyruvate is also converted to acetyl-CoA, an essential metabolite for cell growth. To minimize the competition on pyruvate between bio-production and cell growth, two-stage metabolic switches were used to activate the transcription of pathway genes only in the production phase for several pyruvate-derived chemicals, such as lactate (
                    - link "Hwang et al., 2017" [ref=e1092] [cursor=pointer]:
                      - /url: "#R50"
                    - text: ;
                    - link "Zhou et al., 2012" [ref=e1093] [cursor=pointer]:
                      - /url: "#R142"
                    - text: ), 2,3-butanediol (
                    - link "Hwang et al., 2017" [ref=e1094] [cursor=pointer]:
                      - /url: "#R50"
                    - text: ), and iso-butanol (
                    - link "Zhao et al., 2018" [ref=e1095] [cursor=pointer]:
                      - /url: "#R136"
                    - text: ).
                  - paragraph [ref=e1096]:
                    - text: Slowing down the TCA cycle is another widely used dynamic control strategy. For example, repressing the citrate synthase dynamically has been used to enhance the production of chemicals derived from acetyl-CoA and oxaloacetate, such as isopropanol (
                    - link "Soma et al., 2014" [ref=e1097] [cursor=pointer]:
                      - /url: "#R103"
                    - text: ;
                    - link "Soma and Hanai, 2015" [ref=e1098] [cursor=pointer]:
                      - /url: "#R102"
                    - text: ), poly-β-hydroxybutyrate (
                    - link "Gu et al., 2020" [ref=e1099] [cursor=pointer]:
                      - /url: "#R40"
                    - text: ;
                    - link "He et al., 2017" [ref=e1100] [cursor=pointer]:
                      - /url: "#R48"
                    - text: ), and lysine (
                    - link "Zhou and Zeng, 2015a" [ref=e1101] [cursor=pointer]:
                      - /url: "#R139"
                    - text: ","
                    - link "2015b" [ref=e1102] [cursor=pointer]:
                      - /url: "#R140"
                    - text: ). Similarly, dynamically downregulating the isocitrate dehydrogenase was exploited by using a temperature-dependent system with P
                    - subscript [ref=e1103]: R
                    - text: and the repressor cI857 to increase itaconate production, a cis-aconitate derived product (
                    - link "Harder et al., 2018" [ref=e1104] [cursor=pointer]:
                      - /url: "#R44"
                    - text: ). Reactions in the TCA cycle can also be modulated by the concentration of the substrate in the target pathway. For example, 4-hydroxyisoleucine (4-HIL) is synthesized from isoleucine and α-keto-glutarate. To enhance 4-HIL synthesis, the expression of α-ketoglutarate dehydrogenase complex was repressed by an attenuator when the level of isoleucine is high (
                    - link "Zhang et al., 2018" [ref=e1105] [cursor=pointer]:
                      - /url: "#R134"
                    - text: ) (
                    - link "Fig. 4B" [ref=e1106] [cursor=pointer]:
                      - /url: "#F4"
                    - text: ).
                - generic [ref=e1107]:
                  - heading "4.3. Applications in pathways involving shikimate and aromatics" [level=3] [ref=e1108]
                  - paragraph [ref=e1109]:
                    - text: The shikimate pathway provides the building blocks for the biosynthesis of various aromatics in microbes. To improve the production of shikimate or shikimate-derived aromatics, several metabolic nodes have been regulated by dynamic control strategies. The shikimate pathway starts with the condensation of phosphoenolpyruvate (PEP) and erythrose-4-phosphate. Dynamic downregulation of the pathways from PEP to the TCA cycle through antisense RNAs can increase the flux of the shikimate pathway (
                    - link "Yang et al., 2018" [ref=e1110] [cursor=pointer]:
                      - /url: "#R130"
                    - text: ) (
                    - link "Fig. 4C" [ref=e1111] [cursor=pointer]:
                      - /url: "#F4"
                    - text: ). Shikimate and chorismate are important intermediates in the biosynthesis of aromatic amino acids (AAAs, i.e., phenylalanine, tyrosine, and tryptophan), which are essential to cell growth and many other valuable aromatic acids. Additionally, shikimate is a key ingredient in the formulation of drug Tamiflu for the treatment of influenza. To accumulate shikimate, dynamic control has been used to block the expression of shikimate kinase in the production phase (
                    - link "Gao et al., 2020" [ref=e1112] [cursor=pointer]:
                      - /url: "#R37"
                    - text: ","
                    - link "2019" [ref=e1113] [cursor=pointer]:
                      - /url: "#R38"
                    - text: ;
                    - link "Gupta et al., 2017" [ref=e1114] [cursor=pointer]:
                      - /url: "#R43"
                    - text: ). To enhance the production of other aromatic acids, dynamical control via repressing the conversion of chorismate to AAA biosynthesis and activating the target aromatic acid pathway has shown success in the production of MA (
                    - link "Yang et al., 2018" [ref=e1115] [cursor=pointer]:
                      - /url: "#R130"
                    - text: ) (
                    - link "Fig. 4C" [ref=e1116] [cursor=pointer]:
                      - /url: "#F4"
                    - text: ), PHBA (
                    - link "Williams et al., 2015" [ref=e1117] [cursor=pointer]:
                      - /url: "#R121"
                    - text: ), salicylic acid (
                    - link "Dinh and Prather, 2019" [ref=e1118] [cursor=pointer]:
                      - /url: "#R30"
                    - text: ), and 4-hydroxyphenylacetic acid (
                    - link "Shen et al., 2019" [ref=e1119] [cursor=pointer]:
                      - /url: "#R100"
                    - text: ). Many aromatics are secondary metabolites that can be toxic to the host when accumulated. Dynamic upregulation of the secondary biosynthetic pathway has also been applied to improve the production of these metabolites, such as naringenin (
                    - link "Dinh and Prather, 2019" [ref=e1120] [cursor=pointer]:
                      - /url: "#R30"
                    - text: ), vanillin (
                    - link "Liang et al., 2020" [ref=e1121] [cursor=pointer]:
                      - /url: "#R66"
                    - text: ), and vanillic acid (
                    - link "Lo et al., 2016" [ref=e1122] [cursor=pointer]:
                      - /url: "#R73"
                    - text: ).
                - generic [ref=e1123]:
                  - heading "4.4. Applications in pathways consuming Malonyl-CoA" [level=3] [ref=e1124]
                  - paragraph [ref=e1125]:
                    - text: Malonyl-CoA serves as a rate-limiting precursor in fatty acid chain elongation, and has received much attention as a node for dynamic metabolic engineering for the oleochemical family of compounds (
                    - link "David et al., 2016" [ref=e1126] [cursor=pointer]:
                      - /url: "#R27"
                    - text: ;
                    - link "Xu, 2020" [ref=e1127] [cursor=pointer]:
                      - /url: "#R127"
                    - text: ). In
                    - emphasis [ref=e1128]: E. coli
                    - text: ", malonyl-CoA is synthesized from acetyl-CoA by acetyl-CoA carboxylase (ACC) regulon. However, over-expression of ACC leads to significant toxicity, caused by either depletion of the acetyl-CoA metabolite or due to imbalanced expression of the ACC subunits, thus motivating the engineering of several dynamic control schemes. Sensing of malonyl-CoA is typically achieved through the MRTF FapR, whose binding site"
                    - emphasis [ref=e1129]: fapO
                    - text: can be incorporated into engineered promoters. In one control scheme, an engineered phage P
                    - subscript [ref=e1130]: A1
                    - text: promoter with
                    - emphasis [ref=e1131]: fapO
                    - text: operator was used to form a layered negative metabolic feedback loop, where the expression of ACC was negatively regulated by malonyl-CoA via a LacI intermediate (
                    - link "Liu et al., 2015b" [ref=e1132] [cursor=pointer]:
                      - /url: "#R69"
                    - text: ). The negative metabolic feedback improved both cell growth and FFA production compared to the unregulated strains. In another scheme, two engineered promoters with
                    - emphasis [ref=e1133]: fapO
                    - text: operator were developed, which can be induced and repressed by malonyl-CoA respectively (
                    - link "Xu et al., 2014" [ref=e1134] [cursor=pointer]:
                      - /url: "#R128"
                    - text: ). One promoter was used to activate ACC expression, leading to malonyl-CoA production whenever malonyl-CoA concentration was low, representing a negative metabolic loop. Another promoter was used to drive expression of the downstream fatty acid synthase (FAS) module, leading to FAS expression and malonyl-CoA consumption only when the malonyl-CoA level was high. This control scheme led to oscillatory malonyl-CoA dynamics and an improved fatty acid titer over the uncontrolled strain.
                  - paragraph [ref=e1135]:
                    - text: Downregulating fatty acid synthesis has emerged as a new strategy for producing many other malonyl-CoA-derived chemicals. An endogenous QS system coupled with CRISPRi that simultaneously downregulates key nodes in the TCA cycle, fatty acid and AAA synthesis pathways was applied to enhance rapamycin production in an industrial
                    - emphasis [ref=e1136]: Streptomyces
                    - text: species (
                    - link "Tian et al., 2020" [ref=e1137] [cursor=pointer]:
                      - /url: "#R110"
                    - text: ). In another example, fatty acid synthesis is negatively autoregulated through CRISPRi driven by fatty acid-inducible promoters to increase naringenin production in
                    - emphasis [ref=e1138]: Yarrowia lipolytica
                    - text: (
                    - link "Lv et al., 2020" [ref=e1139] [cursor=pointer]:
                      - /url: "#R74"
                    - text: ) (
                    - link "Fig. 4D" [ref=e1140] [cursor=pointer]:
                      - /url: "#F4"
                    - text: ). A second layer of dynamic control was also developed to further improve genetic stability in long-term naringenin production. In this
                    - emphasis [ref=e1141]: Y. lipolytica
                    - text: strain, 3-isopropylmalate dehydrogenase (LEU2), which is involved in leucine synthesis, was controlled by a naringenin-inducible promoter, which couples the naringenin production with cell growth (
                    - link "Fig. 4D" [ref=e1142] [cursor=pointer]:
                      - /url: "#F4"
                    - text: ).
                - generic [ref=e1143]:
                  - heading "4.5. Applications in pathways producing terpenes and terpenoids" [level=3] [ref=e1144]
                  - paragraph [ref=e1145]:
                    - text: Dynamic control has been widely used in terpene and terpenoid production. The building blocks of terpenes and terpenoids are isopentenyl pyrophosphate (IPP) and dimethylallyl pyrophosphate (DMAPP), which need to be synthesized from either acetyl-CoA through the MVA pathway, or from glyceraldehyde 3-phosphate and pyruvate through the methylerythritol phosphate (MEP) pathway. In
                    - emphasis [ref=e1146]: E. coli
                    - text: ", overexpression of the terpenoid pathway can lead to growth retardation ("
                    - link "Farmer and Liao, 2000" [ref=e1147] [cursor=pointer]:
                      - /url: "#R34"
                    - text: ;
                    - link "Kim et al., 2017" [ref=e1148] [cursor=pointer]:
                      - /url: "#R55"
                    - text: ;
                    - link "Martin et al., 2003" [ref=e1149] [cursor=pointer]:
                      - /url: "#R77"
                    - text: ). Both the MVA (
                    - link "Kim et al., 2017" [ref=e1150] [cursor=pointer]:
                      - /url: "#R55"
                    - text: ) and MEP pathways (
                    - link "Farmer and Liao, 2000" [ref=e1151] [cursor=pointer]:
                      - /url: "#R34"
                    - text: ) have been dynamically activated to improve terpenoid production in
                    - emphasis [ref=e1152]: E. coli
                    - text: . Intermediates of the terpenoid pathway, including 1-hydroxy-2-methyl-2-butenyl 4-diphosphate in the MEP pathway, 3-hydroxy-3-methyl-glutaryl-CoA in the MVA pathway, IPP, and FPP, are also toxic to host cells when accumulated to high concentrations (
                    - link "Li et al., 2017" [ref=e1153] [cursor=pointer]:
                      - /url: "#R63"
                    - text: ;
                    - link "Martin et al., 2003" [ref=e1154] [cursor=pointer]:
                      - /url: "#R77"
                    - text: ). Dynamic control has been used to avoid accumulation of these intermediates (
                    - link "Cui et al., 2019" [ref=e1155] [cursor=pointer]:
                      - /url: "#R25"
                    - text: ;
                    - link "Dahl et al., 2013" [ref=e1156] [cursor=pointer]:
                      - /url: "#R26"
                    - text: ;
                    - link "Shen et al., 2016" [ref=e1157] [cursor=pointer]:
                      - /url: "#R99"
                    - text: ). Additionally, some terpenoids, such as sterol in fungi and undecaprenyl pyrophosphate in
                    - emphasis [ref=e1158]: Bacillus
                    - text: species, are essential to the microbial hosts. Dynamic control has been used to balance the synthesis of both these essential terpenoids and the final products (
                    - link "Cui et al., 2019" [ref=e1159] [cursor=pointer]:
                      - /url: "#R25"
                    - text: ;
                    - link "Peng et al., 2018" [ref=e1160] [cursor=pointer]:
                      - /url: "#R88"
                    - text: ;
                    - link "Scalcinati et al., 2012" [ref=e1161] [cursor=pointer]:
                      - /url: "#R97"
                    - text: ;
                    - link "Xie et al., 2015" [ref=e1162] [cursor=pointer]:
                      - /url: "#R126"
                    - text: ;
                    - link "Yuan and Ching, 2015" [ref=e1163] [cursor=pointer]:
                      - /url: "#R133"
                    - text: ). For example, to increase monoterpene production, FPP synthase (Erg20p) was downregulated by ergosterol through protein degradation tag (
                    - link "Peng et al., 2018" [ref=e1164] [cursor=pointer]:
                      - /url: "#R88"
                    - text: ) (
                    - link "Fig. 4E" [ref=e1165] [cursor=pointer]:
                      - /url: "#F4"
                    - text: ). Synthesis of complex terpenoids, such as Taxol, requires oxygenation, which is often catalyzed by cytochrome P450. However, P450 expression is often burdensome to host cells. A stress-responsive promoter with a transcriptional switch has also been applied to optimize the P450 expression level, resulting in increased titers in oxygenated Taxol precursor (
                    - link "Glasscock et al., 2019" [ref=e1166] [cursor=pointer]:
                      - /url: "#R39"
                    - text: ). For a complex terpenoid pathway, multiple metabolic nodes can be simultaneously regulated, as shown in the production of menaquinone-7 using a bifunctional QS system (
                    - link "Cui et al., 2019" [ref=e1167] [cursor=pointer]:
                      - /url: "#R25"
                    - text: ).
                - generic [ref=e1168]:
                  - heading "4.6. Applications in pathways utilizing xylose" [level=3] [ref=e1169]
                  - paragraph [ref=e1170]:
                    - text: Bioproduction from renewable feedstock has led to developments in the utilization of xylan or xylose as a substrate. Xylan utilization was improved in
                    - emphasis [ref=e1171]: E. coli
                    - text: by engineering both extracellular xylan degradation and intracellular xylose conversion (
                    - link "Gao et al., 2020" [ref=e1172] [cursor=pointer]:
                      - /url: "#R37"
                    - text: ). In this case, extracellular xylan degradation was first improved by tuning the expression of xylanases and xylose transporters. Xylose can be converted to xylulose, which can be further utilized in pentose phosphate pathway to support the cell growth. Xylose can also be converted to xylonate, a high-value platform chemical in industry. To increase xylonate production, a QS system was used to downregulate the xylose isomerase (encoded by
                    - emphasis [ref=e1173]: xylA
                    - text: ) and upregulate xylose dehydrogenase (encoded by
                    - emphasis [ref=e1174]: ccxylB
                    - text: ) once the cells reach a desired density (
                    - link "Fig. 4F" [ref=e1175] [cursor=pointer]:
                      - /url: "#F4"
                    - text: ). Efficient xylose utilization is also hampered by carbon catabolite repression. To solve this problem, a protease-based inverter was used to block glucose utilization in the production phase for xylonate production (
                    - link "Gao et al., 2019" [ref=e1176] [cursor=pointer]:
                      - /url: "#R38"
                    - text: ). Another issue in xylonate production is pH homeostasis. Hydrolysis of xylonolactone to xylonate by lactonase can acidify the cell, impairing cell viability (
                    - link "Nygård et al., 2014" [ref=e1177] [cursor=pointer]:
                      - /url: "#R83"
                    - text: ). To achieve intracellular pH homeostasis, a protease-based oscillator was built to periodically slow down the hydrolysis, which further led to an improved xylonate titer (
                    - link "Gao et al., 2019" [ref=e1178] [cursor=pointer]:
                      - /url: "#R38"
                    - text: ). Xylose has also been used for 1,4-butanediol production in
                    - emphasis [ref=e1179]: E. coli
                    - text: . The pathway from xylose to 1,4-butanediol requires additional reducing equivalents from native metabolism. To delay the competition of reducing equivalents, a QS system was exploited for dynamic control of the 1,4-butanediol biosynthesis pathway (
                    - link "Liu and Lu, 2015" [ref=e1180] [cursor=pointer]:
                      - /url: "#R72"
                    - text: ).
              - generic [ref=e1181]:
                - heading "5. Conclusions" [level=2] [ref=e1182]: 5. Conclusions
                - paragraph [ref=e1183]: Dynamic control has been used for overcoming numerous challenges in metabolic engineering, including toxic metabolite accumulation, unbalanced pathway flux, and production heterogeneity, with new applications still being developed. Theoretical work has elucidated the fundamentals on the dynamics and robustness of controlled metabolic systems and has illustrated the choice of control valves and topology. Synthetic biology has provided delicate tools to construct sensors for detecting changing environments and actuators for precise control of cellular metabolism. Altogether, these models and tools have established a strong foundation for future research in dynamic metabolic engineering.
                - paragraph [ref=e1184]: In the near future, we expect this field to continue to grow, especially along the lines of solving several unaddressed challenges. Firstly, current dynamic metabolic control often relies on extensive trial-and-error for choosing topology and tuning of the control parameters, which is labor-intensive. Although several control topologies may help to improve production, the most optimal topology can be difficult to predict. In addition, improved TRY performance is only seen within a narrow range of parameter settings. Addressing these challenges requires precise control design algorithms, which connect the dynamics of control systems with overall metabolite production metrics to quickly identify the desirable control topology and parameters for experimental implementation. Second, for control systems that are not performing adequately, there is a lack of diagnostic tools for assessing dynamic problems, for example long delays, overshoot, slow dynamics, and oscillations. This lack of diagnostic tools exacerbates the trial-and-error search for good control systems. Third, although a key advantage of implementing dynamic controls is to respond to heterogeneous microenvironments and metabolite heterogeneity, models of control system often do not account for these heterogeneities. These phenomena may be important in the choice of topologies and parameters. Additionally, methods for rapidly assessing the performance of control systems when subjected to these heterogeneities are lacking. Development of models and methods to account for heterogeneity could be critical for the scale-up and commercialization of many bioprocesses, where large heterogeneities exist. Finally, as the most important component of dynamic control, sensors are still lacking for many metabolites of interest, which limits the application of dynamic control. General methods to engineer sensors specific to a metabolite of interest are strongly needed. New design strategies beyond known MRTFs, responsive promoters, and riboswitches, are particularly interesting. Solving these grand challenges will provide exciting avenues for research at the nexus of control theory, systems and synthetic biology, and metabolic engineering.
